do we need to look at other libraries - like  camelot
15:16


Worked for 1m 14s
Yes, I think it’s worth testing other libraries, and camelot is a sensible one to try.

For your use case, I’d treat it as a targeted benchmark, not a rewrite:

Camelot is built specifically for PDF table extraction and supports multiple extraction modes plus parsing metrics. It only works on text-based PDFs, not scanned ones. Camelot docs
pdfplumber is also strong for table extraction and visual debugging, and it exposes page lines/rectangles/text in a way that can help with BOM-heavy engineering drawings. pdfplumber repo/docs
tabula-py is another reasonable benchmark option, especially for table regions/templates, but it adds a Java dependency. tabula-py docs
My recommendation:

yes, test Camelot
also test pdfplumber side by side
use them specifically for BOM/table extraction, not as a replacement for your whole pipeline
The practical approach would be:

keep your existing title-block / geometry / feature logic
add a pluggable BOM extractor layer
benchmark current extractor vs camelot vs pdfplumber on your validation batch
keep whichever wins per drawing type, or use a fallback order
For these manufacturing PDFs, I suspect the best outcome may be:

pdfplumber or current logic for noisy mixed-layout pages
Camelot for cleaner ruled BOM tables
existing custom code for title blocks, part linking, and manufacturing inference
So: yes, worth exploring, but as a controlled experiment on the BOM problem rather than a full library switch. If you want, I can set up that comparison layer next.

ok, can we back track in terms of the sql ? which files do I need to use for that ? Can we revisit that again and how exact i run these and do these go into vs ?
15:43

Yes. The two SQL files you need are:

Schema file:
postgres_scan_store.sql
Data insert file:
drawing_scan_batch_export.sql
What each one does:

postgres_scan_store.sql
creates the database tables and views
drawing_scan_batch_export.sql
inserts the JSON-derived drawing data into those tables
You do not run these in VS Code or Visual Studio unless you happen to use a SQL extension there. The simplest place is:

DBeaver
Run them in this order in DBeaver:

connect to your PostgreSQL database
open postgres_scan_store.sql
run it
open drawing_scan_batch_export.sql
run it
That’s it.

If you want to regenerate the insert SQL from the current JSON folder, run this in PowerShell:

python .\src\main.py --export-json-dir-to-sql "C:\ClaudeVision\output\json"
That recreates:

C:\ClaudeVision\output\sql\drawing_scan_batch_export.sql
So the practical workflow is:

generate JSONs from PDFs
generate batch SQL from JSONs
run schema SQL once in DBeaver
run batch insert SQL in DBeaver
query the tables in DBeaver
The Python code files involved in generating the SQL are:

config.py
file_scan.py
main.py
sql_export.py
But for using the SQL right now, the key files are just:

schema SQL
generated insert SQL
If you want, next I can give you:

the exact DBeaver click-by-click steps
and a few starter SQL queries to check the imported data.




15:43
ok, so I run the contents of this file postgres_scan_store.sql in the DBEAVER databsase manager . for the drawing_scan_batch_export.sql. how and where do I run this ?
15:47

Yes.

Run both files in DBeaver, against the same PostgreSQL database connection.

Order:

run postgres_scan_store.sql
then run drawing_scan_batch_export.sql
So for drawing_scan_batch_export.sql, the answer is:

open that .sql file in DBeaver
make sure it is attached to the same target database/connection
click Execute SQL Script or press Alt+X
that inserts the data into the tables created by the first script
So both scripts are run:

in DBeaver
on the same database
schema first, inserts second
Very simple mental model:

first script creates empty shelves
second script puts the drawing data onto the shelves
Typical DBeaver flow:

open DBeaver
connect to your PostgreSQL database
File > Open File...
choose postgres_scan_store.sql
run it
File > Open File...
choose drawing_scan_batch_export.sql
run it
After that, refresh the database navigator and you should see tables like:

drawing_scan_run
drawing_document
drawing_page
drawing_part
So yes: drawing_scan_batch_export.sql is also run inside DBeaver, not in PowerShell and not in VS.





15:47
But drawing_scan_batch_export.sql file needs ro be updated somehow from the new json files every time ?
15:49


Yes, exactly.

drawing_scan_batch_export.sql is not self-updating. You regenerate it from the current JSON files whenever you want a fresh batch import.

You do that in PowerShell with:

python .\src\main.py --export-json-dir-to-sql "C:\ClaudeVision\output\json"
That recreates:

drawing_scan_batch_export.sql
So the cycle is:

run PDFs to produce/update JSON files
regenerate drawing_scan_batch_export.sql from those JSONs
run that SQL file in DBeaver
So yes:

new JSONs mean you should regenerate the batch export SQL
then rerun the new SQL in DBeaver if you want the database to reflect the latest outputs
If you only added one or two new JSON files, the regenerated batch SQL will include everything in output/json again, so it’s effectively a fresh batch insert script.

Because your schema stores versions, this is okay conceptually, but longer term we may want to make imports smarter so you can:

insert only new versions
avoid duplicate reruns if the same SQL is executed twice
For now though, the correct answer is:

yes, regenerate the SQL file each time from the latest JSONs before importing to DB.
